Two-stage least squares with clustered data
Anqi Zhao, Peng Ding, Fan Li

Abstract
Clustered data—where units of observation are nested within higher-level groups, such as repeated measurements on users, or panel data of firms, industries, or geographic regions—are ubiquitous in business research. When the objective is to estimate the causal effect of a potentially endogenous treatment, a common approach—which we call the canonical two-stage least squares ( 2sls )—is to fit a 2sls regression of the outcome on treatment status with instrumental variables (IVs) for point estimation, and apply cluster-robust standard errors to account for clustering in inference. When both the treatment and IVs vary within clusters, a natural alternative—which we call the two-stage least squares with fixed effects ( 2sfe )—is to include cluster indicators in the 2sls specification, thereby incorporating cluster information in point estimation as well. This paper clarifies the trade-off between these two approaches within the local average treatment effect (LATE) framework, and makes three contributions. First, we establish the validity of both approaches for Wald-type inference of the LATE when clusters are homogeneous, and characterize their relative efficiency. We show that, when the true outcome model includes cluster-specific effects, 2sfe is more efficient than the canonical 2sls only when the variation in cluster-specific effects dominates that in unit-level errors. Second, we show that with heterogeneous clusters, 2sfe recovers a weighted average of cluster-specific LATEs, whereas the canonical 2sls generally does not. Third, to guide empirical choice between the two procedures, we develop a joint asymptotic theory for the two estimators under homogeneous clusters, and propose a Wald-type test for detecting cluster heterogeneity.



Keywords:
Causal inference, Cluster-robust standard error, Fixed effects, Instrumental variable, Local average treatment effect

1
Introduction

Clustered data—where units of observation are nested within higher-level groups—are ubiquitous in business and economic research.
For example, user browsing behavior and purchase histories on online platforms or mobile applications consist of repeated transactions or visits by the same users over time and are naturally clustered at the user level.
Similarly, widely used panel data consist of repeated observations on the same units—such as individuals, firms, industries, or geographic locations—over multiple time periods, and are naturally clustered at the unit level.

When the objective is to estimate the causal effect of a potentially endogenous treatment, a common approach—which we call the
canonical two-stage least squares
(
2sls
)—is to regress the outcome on treatment status and available instrumental variables (IVs) using
2sls
for point estimation, and apply cluster-robust standard errors to account for within-cluster dependence in inference
(
Cameron and
Miller 2015
;
Baum
et al. 2003
;
Atanasov and
Black 2021
)
.
When both the treatment and IV vary within clusters,
a natural alternative—which we call the
two-stage least squares with fixed effects
(
2sfe
)—is to include cluster indicators in the
2sls
specification to account for cluster-specific effects,
thereby incorporating the cluster information in both point estimation and inference
(
Angrist and
Lang 2004
;
Belo
et al. 2014
;
Atanasov and
Black 2021
, see, e.g.,)
.

Despite the popularity of these two approaches, justifications of their validity under cluster sampling within the local average treatment effect (LATE) framework
(
Imbens and
Angrist 1994
)
has been absent from the literature.
This paper fills this gap and makes the following contributions.

First,
we establish the validity and relative efficiency of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
for large-sample Wald-type inference of the LATE when clusters are homogeneous.
The main results are as follows:

(i)

The point estimators from the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
are both consistent and asymptotically normal for estimating the LATE, with the associated cluster-robust standard errors
(
Liang and
Zeger 1986
)
consistent for estimating their respective variances.

(ii)

When the true outcome model includes cluster-specific effects,
2sfe
is more efficient than the canonical
2sls
only when the variation in cluster-specific effects dominates that in unit-level errors.
This cautions against the routine inclusion of cluster indicators in regression specifications without considering efficiency implications.

(iii)

While covariate adjustment does not necessarily improve efficiency with clustered data
(
Su and Ding 2021
)
, we analyze the special case under the above outcome model with cluster-specific effects, and quantify the efficiency gain from adjusting for cluster-constant covariates in the canonical
2sls
.
In contrast,
2sfe
absorbs all cluster-level variation through cluster indicators, precluding the inclusion of cluster-constant covariates.
This suggests an advantage of the canonical
2sls
over
2sfe
when informative cluster-level covariates are available, and again cautions against the routine inclusion of cluster indicators.

Second, we show that with heterogeneous clusters,
2sfe
recovers a weighted average of cluster-specific LATEs, whereas the canonical
2sls
generally does not.
Accordingly, when analyzing data from possibly heterogeneous clusters—such as firms of different sizes, stores in distinct markets, or user cohorts on digital platforms,
2sfe
is a more attractive choice than the canonical
2sls
.

Third, to guide empirical choice between the two procedures,
we develop a joint asymptotic analysis for the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
under homogeneous clusters, and propose a Wald-type test for detecting cluster heterogeneity.
Rejection of the test provides evidence of cross-cluster heterogeneity and suggests the use of
2sfe
rather than the canonical
2sls
.

Notation.

Let
1_{\{\cdot\}}
denote the indicator function.
Let
\rightsquigarrow
denote convergence in distribution, and let
o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
denote a sequence that converges to zero in probability.
For
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
,
\{(v_{i1},\ldots,v_{iK}):v_{ik}\in\mathbb{R}^{p_{k}}\}
, and
\{(w_{i1},\ldots,w_{iL}):w_{il}\in\mathbb{R}^{q_{l}}\}
defined for a population indexed by
i\in\mathcal{I}
, let
\texttt{ols}(u_{i}\sim v_{i1}+\cdots+v_{iK})
denote the ordinary least squares (
ols
) regression of
u_{i}
on the stacked vector
v_{i}=(v_{i1}^{\top},\ldots,v_{iK}^{\top})^{\top}
over
i\in\mathcal{I}
, and let
\texttt{2sls}(u_{i}\sim v_{i1}+\cdots+v_{iK}\mid w_{i1}+\cdots+w_{iL})
denote the
2sls
regression of
u_{i}
on
v_{i}=(v_{i1}^{\top},\ldots,v_{iK}^{\top})^{\top}
over
i\in\mathcal{I}
, instrumented by
w_{i}=(w_{i1}^{\top},\ldots,w_{iL}^{\top})^{\top}
. We allow each
v_{ik}
and
w_{il}
to be a scalar or a vector and use + to denote concatenation of regressors.
Throughout, we use
\texttt{ols}(\cdot)
and
\texttt{2sls}(\cdot)
to denote the numerical outputs of ordinary least squares and two-stage least squares without imposing any assumptions about the corresponding linear model.
For a random variable
Y\in\mathbb{R}
and a random vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let

\proj(Y\mid X)
denote the linear projection of
Y
onto
X
, defined as
\proj(Y\mid X)=b_{0}^{\top}X
, where
b_{0}=\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\E\{(Y-b^{\top}X)^{2}\}=\{\E(XX^{\top})\}^{-1}\E(XY)
.

2
Two 2
SLS
specifications for clustered data

2.1
Clustered data with endogenous treatment

Consider a study with two treatment levels, indexed by
d=0,1
, and a study population of
N
units nested within
G
groups, or
clusters
, indexed by
g=1,\ldots,G
.
Let
n_{g}
denote the size of cluster
g
, with
n_{g}\geq 1
and
\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}=N
.
For a given study population, we view
G
and
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{G})
as fixed design parameters.

Index the
j
th unit within cluster
g
by
gj
.
Let
\mathcal{I}=\{gj:g=1,\ldots,G;\,j=1,\ldots,n_{g}\}
denote the full set of units, and let
\mathcal{I}_{g}=\{gj:j=1,\ldots,n_{g}\}
denote the set of units within cluster
g
, with
\mathcal{I}=\bigcup_{g=1}^{G}\mathcal{I}_{g}
.
For notational convenience, we also use a single index
i
to denote units in
\mathcal{I}
when cluster membership is not of primary interest, with
i=gj
.
Let
{c(i)}
denote the cluster index of unit
i\in\mathcal{I}
, with
{c(i)}=g
for
{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}
.
Let
C_{i}=(1_{\{{c(i)}=1\}},\ldots,1_{\{{c(i)}=G\}})^{\top}
denote the corresponding vector of cluster indicators.

For each unit
i\in\mathcal{I}
, we observe its treatment status
D_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, outcome of interest
Y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, a binary IV
Z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
, and a
p\times 1
vector of baseline covariates
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
The goal is to estimate the causal effect of the treatment on outcome when the treatment status
D_{i}
is endogenous.

2.2
2SLS for clustered data

We now formalize the definitions of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
, along with their corresponding point estimators and cluster-robust standard errors.
Together, these procedures extend the
ols
regression of
Y_{i}
on
D_{i}
and the
ols
regression of
Y_{i}
on
D_{i}
and cluster indicators
C_{i}
—two standard specifications for analyzing clustered data under exogenous treatment
(
Bugni
et al. 2018
)
—to the IV setting.

As a foundation, Definition
1
below reviews the numerical formulation of general
2sls
following
Baum
et al. 2003
.

Definition 1
.

For
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
v_{i},w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
defined for
i\in\mathcal{I}
, let
\texttt{2sls}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})
denote the
2sls
regression of
u_{i}
on
v_{i}
instrumented by
w_{i}
.
Let
\hat{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
denote the estimated coefficient vector of
v_{i}
from
\texttt{2sls}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})
.
Define
r_{i}=u_{i}-v_{i}^{\top}\hat{\beta}
as the residual for unit
i
, and let
r_{[g]}=(r_{g1},\ldots,r_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{g}}
denote the residual vector for cluster
g
.
The cluster-robust covariance of
\hat{\beta}
equals

\widehat{\cov}(\hat{\beta})=(V^{\top}P_{W}V)^{-1}(V^{\top}P_{W}\widehat{\Omega}P_{W}V)(V^{\top}P_{W}V)^{-1},

where
V=(v_{11},\ldots,v_{G,{n_{G}}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times m}
,
P_{W}=W(W^{\top}W)^{-1}W^{\top}
with
W=(w_{11},\ldots,w_{G,{n_{G}}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times m}
, and
\widehat{\Omega}=\diag(r_{[1]}r_{[1]}^{\top},\ldots,r_{[G]}r_{[G]}^{\top})\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
denotes the block diagonal matrix with
\{r_{[g]}r_{[g]}^{\top}:g=1,\ldots,G\}
on the diagonal.
The cluster-robust standard errors of the elements of
\hat{\beta}
are the square roots of the diagonal elements of
\widehat{\cov}(\hat{\beta})
.

Definitions
2
–
3
below formalize the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
following Definition
1
.
We begin with the unadjusted specifications to present our main theory and quantify the impact of covariate adjustment in Section
3.3
.

Definition 2

(Canonical
2sls
)
.

Fit

\texttt{2sls}(Y_{i}\sim 1+D_{i}\mid 1+Z_{i}),

the
2sls
regression of
Y_{i}
on
v_{i}=(1,D_{i})^{\top}
instrumented by
w_{i}=(1,Z_{i})^{\top}
, following Definition
1
.
Let
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
denote the estimated coefficient of
D_{i}
, and let
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}}
denote the corresponding cluster-robust standard error.

The canonical
2sls
in Definition
2
uses no cluster information in the regression specification.
Consequently, clustering is ignored in point estimation and accounted for only through the cluster-robust standard error
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}}
.
As previewed in Section
1
, an alternative is to include cluster indicators
C_{i}=(1_{\{{c(i)}=1\}},\ldots,1_{\{{c(i)}=G\}})^{\top}
in the
2sls
specification, thereby accounting for systematic differences across clusters in point estimation as well. We call this procedure
two-stage least squares with fixed effects
(
2sfe
), as formalized in Definition
3
below.

Definition 3

(Two-stage least squares with fixed effects (
2sfe
))
.

Fit

\texttt{2sls}(Y_{i}\sim D_{i}+C_{i}\mid Z_{i}+C_{i}),

the
2sls
regression of
Y_{i}
on
v_{i}=(D_{i},C_{i}^{\top})^{\top}
instrumented by
w_{i}=(Z_{i},C_{i}^{\top})^{\top}
, following Definition
1
.
Let
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
denote the estimated coefficient of
D_{i}
, and let
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}}
denote the corresponding cluster-robust standard error.

The
2sfe
accounts for clustering through both the inclusion of
C_{i}
in the regression specification and the use of the cluster-robust standard error
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}}
.
The coefficients of
C_{i}
are commonly interpreted as cluster fixed effects in regression-based fixed-effects estimation
(
Wooldridge 2010
)
.
As a limitation,
2sfe
is well defined only if both
Z_{i}
and
D_{i}
vary within at least one cluster. It is therefore not applicable in settings where either the treatment, the IV, or both are constant within clusters.
A notable example is randomized cluster experiments with noncompliance
(
Frangakis
et al. 2002
;
Jo et al. 2008
;
Agbla
et al. 2020
)
, in which the random treatment assignment serves as a cluster-constant IV.

Remark 1
.

Let
\dot{Y}_{i}=Y_{i}-\bar{Y}_{{c(i)}}
denote the cluster-centered variant of
Y_{i}
, where
\bar{Y}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}Y_{i}
is the within-cluster average of
Y_{i}
for cluster
g
. Similarly, define
\dot{D}_{i}
and
\dot{Z}_{i}
as the cluster-centered variants of
D_{i}
and
Z_{i}
.
The Frisch–Waugh–Lovell type results for
2sls
in
Basu 2024
imply that
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}})
equal the coefficient of
\dot{D}_{i}
and associated cluster-robust standard error in the
2sls
regression of
\dot{Y}_{i}
on
\dot{D}_{i}
instrumented by
\dot{Z}_{i}
.
This ensures that
2sfe
remains computationally feasible even with a large number of clusters.

2.3
Assumptions under the LATE framework

We define the sampling and IV assumptions using the potential outcomes framework
(
Imbens and
Angrist 1994
;
Angrist
et al. 1996
)
.
For
z,d=0,1
,
let
D_{i}(z)
denote the potential treatment status of unit
i
if
Z_{i}=z
, and
let
Y_{i}(z,d)
denote the potential outcome of unit
i
if
(Z_{i},D_{i})=(z,d)
.
The observed treatment status and outcome satisfy
D_{i}=D_{i}(Z_{i})
and
Y_{i}=Y_{i}(Z_{i},D_{i})=Y_{i}(Z_{i},D_{i}(Z_{i}))
.
Following standard terminology, we classify the units into four compliance types, denoted by
U_{i}
, based on the joint values of
D_{i}(1)
and
D_{i}(0)
. We call unit
i

an
always-taker
if
D_{i}(1)=D_{i}(0)=1
, denoted by
U_{i}=\textup{a}
;
a
complier
if
D_{i}(1)=1
and
D_{i}(0)=0
, denoted by
U_{i}=\textup{c}
;
a
defier
if
D_{i}(1)=0
and
D_{i}(0)=1
, denoted by
U_{i}=\textup{d}
; and
a
never-taker
if
D_{i}(1)=D_{i}(0)=0
, denoted by
U_{i}=\textup{n}
.

Let
\mathcal{Q}_{i}=\{Y_{i}(z,d),D_{i}(z),Z_{i},X_{i}:z,d=0,1\}
denote the collection of all variables associated with unit
i
, and let
\mathcal{Q}_{{[g]}}=\{\mathcal{Q}_{i}:{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\}
denote the corresponding collection for cluster
g
.
Assumption
1
below states the standard
cluster sampling
assumption
(
Wooldridge 2010
;
Hansen and
Lee 2019
)
, requiring independence across clusters while allowing arbitrary dependence within each cluster.

Assumption 1

(Cluster sampling)
.

\mathcal{Q}_{{[g]}}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{[g^{\prime}]}
are independent for
g\neq g^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,G\}
.

Assumption
2
below states the IV assumptions for clustered data.

Assumption 2

(IV assumptions for clustered data)
.

For
i\in\mathcal{I}
, the following holds:

(i)

Exclusion restriction:

Y_{i}(0,d)=Y_{i}(1,d)=Y_{i}(d)
for
d=0,1
, where
Y_{i}(d)
denotes the common value.

(ii)

Cluster-level random assignment:

\displaystyle\left\{Z_{i}:{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\right\}\perp\!\!\!\perp\left\{Y_{i}(z,d),D_{i}(z),X_{i}:z,d=0,1;\ {i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\right\}\quad\text{for}\ \ g=1,\ldots,G.

(iii)

Overlap:

0<\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=1)<1
.

(iv)

Relevance:

\E\{D_{i}(1)-D_{i}(0)\}\neq 0
.

(v)

Monotonicity:

D_{i}(1)\geq D_{i}(0)
.

Assumption
2
follows the classical IV assumptions in
Angrist
et al. 1996
except Assumption
2
(
ii
).
Unlike its classical counterpart
Z_{i}\perp\!\!\!\perp\{Y_{i}(z,d),D_{i}(z),X_{i}:z,d=0,1\}
,
which requires random assignment at the unit level, Assumption
2
(
ii
) requires random assignment of the IV at the cluster level,
such that the IV of a unit is independent not only of its own potential treatment status and outcomes but also of those of other units within the same cluster.
Aside from this,
Assumption
2
(
ii
) imposes no restrictions on the joint distribution of
\{Z_{i}:{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\}
within each cluster.
It therefore accommodates both (i) IVs that are independent within clusters and (ii) cluster-level IVs that are constant within clusters.

Assumptions
3
–
4
below state two common, alternative assumptions on the marginal distributions of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
across clusters.
First, Assumption
3
requires that all units share the same marginal distribution.
This assumption holds under a
two-stage sampling
design, also known as
grouped sampling

(
Arkhangelsky and
Imbens 2024
)
, in which (i) the first stage randomly samples
G
clusters from a superpopulation of clusters; (ii) the second stage randomly samples
n_{g}
units from each sampled cluster
g
.
Assumption
4
relaxes Assumption
3
by allowing the marginal distribution of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
to vary across clusters.
We refer to Assumptions
3
and
4
as
homogeneous-clusters
and
heterogeneous-clusters
assumptions, respectively.

Assumption 3

(Homogeneous clusters)
.

\{\mathcal{Q}_{i}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are identically distributed.

Assumption 4

(Heterogeneous clusters)
.

For each
g\in\{1,\ldots,G\}
,
\{\mathcal{Q}_{gj}:j=1,\ldots,n_{g}\}
are identically distributed within the cluster, whereas
\mathcal{Q}_{gj}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{g^{\prime}j^{\prime}}
may differ in distribution for
g\neq g^{\prime}
.

We assume Assumptions
1
–
3
in Section
3
to develop theory under homogeneous clusters, and discuss relaxations of Assumption
3
to Assumption
4
in Section
4
.

3
Theory under homogeneous clusters

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
throughout this section. Recall that
Y_{i}(d)
denotes the common value of
Y_{i}(0,d)=Y_{i}(1,d)
under exclusion restriction in Assumption
2
(
i
).
Assumption
2
(
iv
)–(
v
) (relevance, monotonicity) further imply that
\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{c})>0
.
Define
\tau_{i}=Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)
as the individual treatment effect of unit
i
, and define

\tau_{\textup{c}}=\E(\tau_{i}\mid U_{i}=\textup{c})

(1)

as the
local average treatment effect
(LATE) on compliers under Assumptions
2
–
3
.
We establish below the validity and relative efficiency of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
for estimating
\tau_{\textup{c}}
.

3.1
Validity of Wald-type inference

Proposition
1
below establishes the central limit theorems for
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}})
and
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}})
under Assumptions
1
–
3
, thereby confirming the validity of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
for large-sample Wald-type inference of the LATE with homogeneous clusters.
We adopt the asymptotic framework of
Hansen and
Lee 2019
, as reviewed in Assumption
5
below.
The framework requires each cluster to be asymptotically negligible, implying that the number of clusters
G
diverges.
Beyond this, it allows for unequal and diverging cluster sizes
n_{g}
.

Assumption 5
.

As
N\to\infty
,
\displaystyle\max_{g=1,\ldots,G}\,{n_{g}^{2}}/N\to 0
,
{\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}}\,N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}^{2}<\infty
.

Proposition 1
.

As
N\to\infty
, under Assumptions
1
–
3
,
5
, and proper moment and rank conditions, we have

(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\tau_{\textup{c}})/\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1),\qquad(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}-\tau_{\textup{c}})/\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Proposition
1
establishes the consistency and asymptotic normality of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
for estimating
\tau_{\textup{c}}
, and justifies using the cluster-robust standard errors for estimating their corresponding variances.
We provide the formal moment and rank conditions in Theorem
3
in Section
4
, where we establish the joint asymptotic distribution of
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}},\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}})
for testing possible violations of Assumption
3
.
The central limit theorem for
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
is a special case of the general theory for generalized method of moments estimators
(
Hansen and
Lee 2019
)
.
Establishing the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
requires additional technical arguments, as the dimension of
C_{i}
diverges.

3.2
Efficiency comparison

We now compare the relative efficiency of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
.
In summary, when the true outcome model includes cluster-specific effects,
2sfe
is more efficient than the canonical
2sls
only when the variation in cluster-specific effects dominates that in unit-level errors.
These findings caution against the routine inclusion of cluster indicators in regression specifications without evaluating the associated efficiency trade-offs, particularly when informative cluster-level covariates are available.

3.2.1
Potential outcomes model for comparing efficiency

Assumption
6
below specifies a model for the potential outcomes that we use to study relative efficiency.

Assumption 6
.

For
i\in\mathcal{I}
, the potential outcomes satisfy

\displaystyle Y_{i}(d)=d\tau+\alpha_{{c(i)}}+\epsilon_{i}\quad\text{for}\ \ d=0,1,

(2)

where

(i)

\tau\in\mathbb{R}
is an unknown constant;

(ii)

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{G}\in\mathbb{R}
are unobserved cluster-specific effects, with
\E(\alpha_{g})=\mu_{\alpha}
and
\var(\alpha_{g})=\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}
for unknown constants
(\mu_{\alpha},\sigma^{2}_{\alpha})
;

(iii)

\{\epsilon_{i}\in\mathbb{R}:i\in\mathcal{I}\}
are unobserved unit-level errors that satisfy the following properties: Let
\epsilon_{{[g]}}=(\epsilon_{g1},\ldots,\epsilon_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}=(\epsilon_{i}:{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}})
denote the
n_{g}\times 1
vector of
\epsilon_{i}
within cluster
g
.

-

\epsilon_{[1]},\ldots,\epsilon_{[G]}
are independent;

-

\E(\epsilon_{{[g]}}\mid\alpha_{g})=0_{n_{g}}
and
\cov(\epsilon_{{[g]}}\mid\alpha_{g})=\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}I_{n_{g}}
for an unknown constant
\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}>0
.

Assumption
6
has several implications.
First, it implies that
\tau_{i}=Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)=\tau
is constant for
i\in\mathcal{I}
, so that the LATE satisfies
\tau_{\textup{c}}=\tau
.
In addition, (
2
) implies that the observed outcome satisfies

Y_{i}=D_{i}\tau+\alpha_{{c(i)}}+\epsilon_{i},

which coincides with the functional form of the fixed effects model
(
Wooldridge 2010
;
Arkhangelsky and
Imbens 2024
)
.
However, let
D_{{[g]}}=(D_{g1},\ldots,D_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}
denote the treatment vector for cluster
g
. The fixed effects model further requires that

\E(\epsilon_{{[g]}}\mid D_{{[g]}},\alpha_{g})=0\quad\text{for}\ \ g=1,\ldots,G

(3)

in addition to Assumption
6
. In contrast, Assumption
6
imposes no restrictions on the dependence between
D_{{[g]}}
and
\{\alpha_{g},\epsilon_{{[g]}}:g=1,\ldots,G\}
.
When (
3
) is violated, standard fixed effects estimation via the
ols
regression of
Y_{i}
on
(D_{i},C_{i})
may be inconsistent.
In contrast, Proposition
1
implies that both
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
are consistent for
\tau
regardless of whether (
3
) or Assumption
6
holds.
We establish below their relative efficiency under Assumption
6
.

3.2.2
Main results

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
and
6
throughout this subsection.
Let
\sigma^{2}_{Z}=\var(Z_{i})
and
\pi_{\textup{c}}=\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{c})
under Assumption
3
, with
\sigma^{2}_{Z},\pi_{\textup{c}}>0
under Assumption
2
.
Let

S_{Z,\textup{in}}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{{c(i)}})^{2}

denote the sample within-cluster variance of
Z_{i}
.
Under Assumptions
2
–
3
, define

\displaystyle\kappa_{N}=\E(S_{Z,\textup{in}})/\sigma^{2}_{Z}

(4)

as a standardized version of
\E(S_{Z,\textup{in}})
, and let

c_{N}=N\var(\bar{Z})/\sigma^{2}_{Z}.

Intuitively,
\kappa_{N}
captures the within-cluster variance of
Z_{i}
, while
c_{N}
reflects the deviation of
N\var(\bar{Z})
from
\sigma^{2}_{Z}
due to within-cluster dependence.
Under cluster sampling,
\kappa_{N}
and
c_{N}
generally depend on the sample size
N
, and may not converge as
N\to\infty
without assumptions beyond Assumptions
1
–
3
. In addition, we can show that

\kappa_{N}\in[0,1],

(5)

where

(i)

\kappa_{N}=0
if and only if
Z_{i}
is constant within each cluster, so that
2sfe
is degenerate;

(ii)

\kappa_{N}=1
if and only if
\var(\bar{Z}_{g})=0
for all
g
, so that the IV proportion
\bar{Z}_{g}
is fixed within each cluster;

see (
11
) below for details.
Theorem
1
below quantifies the probability limits of
\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}
under Assumption
6
, and establishes conditions for efficiency comparison.
To simplify the presentation, we assume
\inf_{N}\kappa_{N}>0
, so that
2sfe
is nondegenerate for all
N
.

Theorem 1
.

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
,
5
–
6
, and proper moment and rank conditions that ensure
\inf_{N}\kappa_{N}>0
.
Then

N\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}=v_{\textup{2sls},N}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\quad N\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}=v_{\textup{2sfe},N}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\quad\dfrac{\var(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}})}{\var(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}})}=\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

(6)

where

v_{\textup{2sls},N}=\dfrac{1}{\sigma^{2}_{Z}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c}}^{2}}\left(\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}+\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}\cdot c_{N}\right),\quad v_{\textup{2sfe},N}=\dfrac{1}{\kappa_{N}\cdot\sigma^{2}_{Z}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c}}^{2}}\cdot\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon},

(7)

with

\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}=\kappa_{N}\left(1+\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}\cdot c_{N}\right).

(8)

In particular,

(i)

\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}>1
if and only if

\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}>\dfrac{1-\kappa_{N}}{\kappa_{N}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{c_{N}}.

(9)

(ii)

v_{\textup{2sls},N}=v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
if
\var(\bar{Z}_{g})=0
for all
g
, i.e., the IV proportion
\bar{Z}_{g}
is fixed within each cluster.

Proposition
1
implies that
\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}
are consistent for estimating the variances of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
, respectively.
Therefore, (
6
) justifies viewing
v_{\textup{2sls},N}
and
v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
as approximations to the asymptotic variances of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
, denoted by
\vara(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}})
and
\vara(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}})
, respectively, under Assumption
6
. Consequently, the ratio
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
approximates the asymptotic relative efficiency.

From (
7
), the asymptotic variance of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
,
\vara(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}})\approx v_{\textup{2sls},N}
, depends on both
\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
and
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}
, whereas that of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
,
\vara(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}})\approx v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
, depends only on
\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
.

From (
5
) and (
8
), the ratio
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
for asymptotic relative efficiency can be either greater or less than 1, implying that
2sfe
—with additional cluster indicators in the regression specification—does not necessarily improve efficiency relative to the canonical
2sls
.
In particular, (
8
) implies that
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
depends on both

•

\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
, which characterizes the variation in cluster-specific effects relative to that in unit-level errors in the potential outcomes model under Assumption
6
, and

•

(\kappa_{N},c_{N})
, which are determined by the IV assignment mechanism.

We discuss their respective impacts below.

Impact of
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
on
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
.

The expression of
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
in (
8
) increases with
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
. Therefore, greater variation in cluster-specific effects relative to that in unit-level errors indicates greater efficiency of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
relative to
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
.
In particular, (
9
) provides a lower bound on
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
that ensures the asymptotic efficiency of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
relative to
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
.
As two extreme cases:

•

The classical fixed effects model views fixed effects as arbitrary fixed numbers
(
Raudenbush and
Bryk 2002
)
, which is equivalent to assuming
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}\approx\infty
in Assumption
6
.
From this perspective, the condition in (
9
) necessarily holds, implying the asymptotic efficiency of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
relative to
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
.
In this setting, clusters can also be viewed as heterogeneous with distinct outcome distributions; see Assumption
4
and Section
4
.

•

When
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}=0
, it follows from (
8
) that
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}=\kappa_{N}\leq 1
, where the equality holds if and only if the IV proportion
\bar{Z}_{g}
is fixed within each cluster. This implies that
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
reduces efficiency in the absence of cluster-specific effects, as formalized in Corollary
1
below.

Corollary 1
.

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
,
5
–
6
, and proper moment and rank conditions that ensure
\inf_{N}\kappa_{N}>0
. If
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}=0
, then

\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}/\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}=\kappa_{N}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
,
where
\kappa_{N}\in(0,1]
and
\kappa_{N}=1
if and only if the IV proportion
\bar{Z}_{g}
is fixed within each cluster.

Impact of IV assignment mechanism on
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
.

Let

\phi_{g}=n_{g}^{-2}\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\corr(Z_{i},Z_{i^{\prime}})\in[0,1]

(10)

denote the average pairwise correlation of
Z_{i}
within cluster
g
. Under Assumptions
1
–
3
, we have

\phi_{g}=\var(\bar{Z}_{g})/\sigma^{2}_{Z},

so that (i)
\phi_{g}=0
if and only if the IV proportion
\bar{Z}_{g}
is fixed, corresponding to the most negatively correlated assignment of
Z_{i}
within cluster
g
; and (ii)
\phi_{g}=1
if and only if
Z_{i}
is constant within cluster
g
, corresponding to the most positively correlated assignment that causes
2sfe
to be degenerate.

We show in Lemma
S11
in the Supplementary Material that

\kappa_{N}=1-N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}\phi_{g}\in[0,1],\qquad c_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}^{2}\phi_{g}\in\left[0,N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}^{2}\right],

(11)

which implies (
5
) and the subsequent conditions for equality.
Together, (
7
) and (
11
) imply that both
v_{\textup{2sls},N}
and
v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
increase with the average within-cluster correlations
\phi_{g}
, so that the overall impact of
\phi_{g}
on
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
is indeterminate.
The assumption that
\inf_{N}\kappa_{N}>0
rules out the scenario in which
\phi_{g}=1
for all
g
.
When
\phi_{g}=0
for all
g
, so that
\bar{Z}_{g}
is fixed within each cluster,
(
11
) implies that
(\kappa_{N},c_{N})=(1,0)
, so that
v_{\textup{2sls},N}=v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
from (
8
), as formalized in Theorem
1
(
ii
).

We provide in Corollary
2
below intuition for two additional special cases corresponding to (i) equal-sized clusters, as in panel data where all
n_{g}
equal the number of observation periods, and (ii) IVs that are independently assigned within clusters.

Let
\bar{n}=N/G
denote the average cluster size, and let
\bar{\phi}=G^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\phi_{g}
with
\bar{\phi}\in[0,1]
.

Corollary 2
.

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
and
6
.

(i)

When the clusters are of equal sizes with
n_{g}=\bar{n}
for all
g
, we have

\displaystyle\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}=(1-\bar{\phi})\left(1+\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}\cdot\bar{n}\bar{\phi}\right),

with

\displaystyle\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}>1

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}>\dfrac{1}{\bar{n}(1-\bar{\phi})},\quad\bar{\phi}\neq 0

(12)

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle 0<\bar{\phi}<1-\bar{n}^{-1}\cdot\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}.

(ii)

When
\corr(Z_{i},Z_{i^{\prime}})=0
for all
i\neq i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}
within the same cluster, we have

\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}=(1-\bar{n}^{-1})\left(1+\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}\right),

with

\dfrac{v_{\textup{2sls},N}}{v_{\textup{2sfe},N}}>1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}>\dfrac{1}{\bar{n}-1}.

(13)

Corollary
2
(
i
) provides the simplified expression of
v_{\textup{2sls},N}/v_{\textup{2sfe},N}
when clusters are of equal sizes, along with the corresponding threshold for
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}
under which
2sfe
improves asymptotic efficiency.
The second sufficient and necessary condition in (
12
) implies that smaller within-cluster correlation of
Z_{i}
facilitates efficiency gain for
2sfe
.
Corollary
2
(
ii
) provides the corresponding results when
Z_{i}
are uncorrelated within clusters. A special case is when the IVs are independently assigned within each cluster.
The sufficient and necessary condition in (
13
) implies that larger cluster sizes facilitate efficiency gain for
2sfe
.

3.3
Impact of covariate adjustment

We now examine the impact of covariate adjustment.
Theory of least squares suggests that covariate adjustment does not necessarily ensure efficiency gain with clustered data
(
Su and Ding 2021
)
.
We investigate the special case in which the potential outcomes model satisfies Assumption
6
, and establish the efficiency gain from adjusting for cluster-constant covariates in the canonical
2sls
.
Note that
2sfe
precludes the inclusion of cluster-constant covariates.
This highlights an advantage of the canonical
2sls
over
2sfe
when informative cluster-level covariates are available.

Recall that
X_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
denotes the vector of baseline covariates for
i\in\mathcal{I}
.
Define

\displaystyle\texttt{2sls}(Y_{i}\sim 1+D_{i}+X_{i}\mid 1+Z_{i}+X_{i}),

(14)

\displaystyle\texttt{2sls}(Y_{i}\sim D_{i}+C_{i}+X_{i}\mid Z_{i}+C_{i}+X_{i})

(15)

as the covariate-adjusted variants of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
following Definition
1
, and denote by
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}},r_{i,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}})
and
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}},r_{i,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}})
the coefficients of
D_{i}
, corresponding cluster-robust standard errors, and residuals, respectively.
As a basis, Proposition
2
below generalizes Proposition
1
and establishes the validity of
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}})
and
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}},\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}})
for large-sample Wald-type inference of the LATE under arbitrary potential outcomes models.

Proposition 2
.

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
,
5
, and proper moment and rank conditions, Then

\displaystyle(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}-\tau_{\textup{c}})/\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1),\quad(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}-\tau_{\textup{c}})/\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1).

We now examine the impact of covariate adjustment on efficiency when the potential outcomes model satisfies Assumption
6
.
As a basis, Assumption
7
below augments Assumption
6
(
iii
) to require exogeneity of
X_{i}
conditional on the cluster-specific effects.

Assumption 7
.

In the setting of Assumption
6
, further assume

\E(\epsilon_{{[g]}}\mid X_{{[g]}},\alpha_{g})=0_{n_{g}}
and
\cov(\epsilon_{{[g]}}\mid X_{{[g]}},\alpha_{g})=\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}I_{n_{g}}
, where
X_{{[g]}}=\{X_{i}:{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\}
.

Proposition
3
below formalizes the impact of adjusting for cluster-constant covariates on the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
.

Proposition 3
.

Assume Assumptions
1
–
3
,
5
–
7
, and proper moment and rank conditions.
Assume that
X_{i}
is constant within clusters, and let
X^{*}_{g}
denote the common value of
X_{i}
within cluster
g
.
Then the covariate-adjusted
2sfe
in (
15
) reduces to the covariate-free
2sfe
in Definition
3
with
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}^{2}=\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}
, while the cluster-robust standard error from the covariate-adjusted canonical
2sls
satisfies

\displaystyle\dfrac{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}}{\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-\var\{\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}\cdot\dfrac{c_{N}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}+\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}\cdot c_{N}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

\displaystyle\dfrac{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}}{\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[1-\var\{\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}\cdot\dfrac{c_{N}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}+\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}\cdot c_{N}}\right]\cdot\kappa_{N}\left(1+\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}\cdot c_{N}\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

where
\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})
denotes the linear projection of
\alpha_{g}
onto
(1,X^{*}_{g})
, with
\var\{\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}\in[0,\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}]
. In particular,

(i)

when
\var\{\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}=0
, we have

\dfrac{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}}{\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}}=1+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\quad\dfrac{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}}{\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}}=\kappa_{N}\left(1+\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}\cdot c_{N}\right)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

(ii)

when
\var\{\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}=\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}
, we have

\dfrac{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}}{\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}}=\dfrac{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}}{\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}+\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}\cdot c_{N}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\quad\dfrac{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}}{\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}}=\kappa_{N}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1).

Proposition
3
implies that the variance reduction for the canonical
2sls
from adjusting for
X_{i}=X^{*}_{g}
results from the portion of variation in
\alpha_{g}
that can be explained by
X^{*}_{g}
.
In particular, the projection of
\alpha_{g}
onto
(1,X^{*}_{g})
, denoted by
\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})
, represents the part in
\alpha_{g}
that is explained by
X^{*}_{g}
, with

\var\{\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}=\var(\alpha_{g})-\var\{\alpha_{g}-\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})\}\in[0,\var(\alpha_{g})].

When
\proj(\alpha_{g}\mid 1,X^{*}_{g})=0
, so that
X^{*}_{g}
explains no variation in
\alpha_{g}
, there is no efficiency gain, and the covariate-adjusted canonical
2sls
is equivalent to its unadjusted counterpart. The efficiency comparison between
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
reduces to the results in Theorem
1
.
Conversely, when
\alpha_{g}
is fully explained by
X^{*}_{g}
, the ratio
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}^{2}/\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}
approaches
\kappa_{N}\leq 1
, implying asymptotic efficiency of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}
over
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
.

4
Theory under heterogeneous clusters

The discussion so far assumes identical distributions of
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
across all units under Assumption
3
.
In this section, we establish the implications of cross-cluster heterogeneity under Assumption
4
, and propose a Wald-type test for cluster heterogeneity.

4.1
Properties of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
under heterogeneous clusters

Assume Assumptions
1
–
2
and
4
throughout this subsection.
In the absence of Assumption
3
, the LATE
\tau_{\textup{c}}
in (
1
) is no longer well defined.
Instead, define

\tau_{\textup{c},g}=\E(\tau_{gj}\mid U_{gj}=\textup{c})

(16)

as the
cluster-specific LATE
for cluster
g=1,\ldots,G
,
where
\E(\cdot)
denotes the expectation with respect to the common marginal distribution of units
\{gj:j=1,\ldots,n_{g}\}
in cluster
g
. We establish in Theorem
2
below the probability limits of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
as functions of
\tau_{\textup{c},g}
.

For
g=1,\ldots,G
, let

e_{g}=\mathbb{P}(Z_{gj}=1),\quad\sigma_{Z,g}^{2}=\var(Z_{gj})=e_{g}(1-e_{g}),\quad\pi_{\textup{c},g}=\mathbb{P}(U_{gj}=\textup{c})

denote the cluster-specific counterparts of
e=\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=1)
,
\sigma^{2}_{Z}=\var(Z_{i})
, and
\pi_{\textup{c}}=\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{c})
under Assumption
3
.
Let
\mu_{Y(0),g}
,
\pi_{\textup{a},g}
, and
\tau_{\textup{a},g}
denote the common values of
\E\{Y_{gj}(0)\}
,
\mathbb{P}(U_{gj}=\textup{a})
, and
\E(\tau_{gj}\mid U_{gj}=\textup{a})
across
j=1,\ldots,n_{g}
for units within cluster
g
.
Recall that
\phi_{g}=n_{g}^{-2}\sum_{i,i^{\prime}\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}\corr(Z_{i},Z_{i^{\prime}})
as defined in (
10
).

Theorem 2
.

As
N\to\infty
, if Assumptions
1
–
2
and
4
hold,
\displaystyle\max_{g=1,\ldots,G}n_{g}/N\to 0
, and
\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(Y_{i}^{2})<\infty
,
then

\begin{array}[]{l}\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}=\dfrac{\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}e_{g}(\widetilde{\mu}_{g}+\pi_{\textup{c},g}\tau_{\textup{c},g})-N^{-1}\left(\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}e_{g}\right)\left\{\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}(\widetilde{\mu}_{g}+e_{g}\pi_{\textup{c},g}\tau_{\textup{c},g})\right\}}{\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}e_{g}(\pi_{\textup{a},g}+\pi_{\textup{c},g})-N^{-1}\left(\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}e_{g}\right)\left\{\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}(\pi_{\textup{a},g}+e_{g}\pi_{\textup{c},g})\right\}}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\\
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}=\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\kappa_{g,\textup{2sfe}}\cdot\tau_{\textup{c},g}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\end{array}

where
\widetilde{\mu}_{g}=\mu_{Y(0),g}+\pi_{\textup{a},g}\tau_{\textup{a},g}
and

\kappa_{g,\textup{2sfe}}=\dfrac{n_{g}(1-\phi_{g})\sigma_{Z,g}^{2}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c},g}}{\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}(1-\phi_{g})\sigma_{Z,g}^{2}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c},g}}

(17)

with
\kappa_{g,\textup{2sfe}}\in[0,1]
and
\sum_{g=1}^{G}\kappa_{g,\textup{2sfe}}=1
.
Further assume that
e_{g}=e
for all
g
. Then

\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}=\sum_{g=1}^{G}\kappa_{g,\textup{2sls}}\cdot\tau_{\textup{c},g}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\quad\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}=\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\kappa_{g,\textup{2sfe}}\cdot\tau_{\textup{c},g}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\end{array}

where

\kappa_{g,\textup{2sls}}=\dfrac{n_{g}\pi_{\textup{c},g}}{\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}\pi_{\textup{c},g}},\quad\kappa_{g,\textup{2sfe}}=\dfrac{n_{g}(1-\phi_{g})\pi_{\textup{c},g}}{\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}(1-\phi_{g})\pi_{\textup{c},g}}

with
\kappa_{g,*}\in[0,1]
and
\sum_{g=1}^{G}\kappa_{g,*}=1
for
*=\textup{2sls, 2sfe}
.

Theorem
2
has two implications. First, when clusters are heterogeneous,
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
recovers a weighted average of the cluster-specific LATEs
\{\tau_{\textup{c},g}:g=1,\ldots,G\}
, with nonnegative weights that sum to one, whereas
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
generally does not.
This result generalizes the distinction between
\texttt{ols}(Y_{i}\sim 1+D_{i})
and
\texttt{ols}(Y_{i}\sim D_{i}+C_{i})
in analyzing stratified randomized experiments
(
Bugni
et al. 2018
)
to
2sls
, and highlights an advantage of including fixed effects when analyzing data nested within heterogeneous clusters. For example, stores in different geographic locations or firms of differing sizes can form heterogeneous clusters.

Next, as a special case, when the heterogeneous clusters have equal IV probabilities
e_{g}
,
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
also converges in probability to a weighted average of
\{\tau_{\textup{c},g}:g=1,\ldots,G\}
, with nonnegative weights that sum to one. However, these weights generally differ from those of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
.
One example is randomized encouragement experiments in which the encouragement, serving as the IV, is randomly assigned to all units with equal probabilities
(
Angrist
et al. 1996
;
Hoffmann
et al. 2025
)
.

4.2
Test for cluster heterogeneity

Theorem
2
establishes an advantage of the two-stage least squares with fixed effects (
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
) over the canonical
2sls
(
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
) when analyzing heterogeneous clusters.
To facilitate empirical choice between the two procedures,
we propose below a test for possible violations of Assumption
3
, motivated by the difference in the probability limits of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
in Theorem
2
.
Rejection of the test provides evidence of cross-cluster heterogeneity and suggests the use of
2sfe
rather than the canonical
2sls
.

As the theoretical basis,
Theorem
3
below establishes the central limit theorem for the joint distribution of
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}},\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}})
when Assumption
3
holds.

Let
\bar{D}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}D_{i}
denote the average of
D_{i}
over all units, and let
\bar{D}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}D_{i}
denote the within-cluster average for cluster
g
.
Similarly, define
\bar{Z}
and
\bar{Z}_{g}
as the overall and within-cluster averages of
Z_{i}
.
Let
r_{i,\textup{2sls}}
and
r_{i,\textup{2sfe}}
denote the residuals from the canonical
2sls
in Definition
2
and the
2sfe
in Definition
3
, respectively. We show in Theorems
S1
–
S2
in the Supplementary Material that

\displaystyle\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}=\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{S_{ZD}^{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})r_{i,\textup{2sls}}\right\}^{2},

\displaystyle\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}=\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{S_{ZD,\textup{in}}^{2}}\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})r_{i,\textup{2sfe}}\right\}^{2},

where
S_{ZD}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})(D_{i}-\bar{D})
and
S_{ZD,\textup{in}}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{{c(i)}})(D_{i}-\bar{D}_{{c(i)}})
denote the sample covariance and sample within-cluster covariance of
(Z_{i},D_{i})
, respectively, normalized by
N
.
Let

\widehat{\Sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}&\hat{\sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}}\\
\hat{\sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}}&\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}\end{pmatrix},

where

\hat{\sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}}=\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{S_{ZD}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{S_{ZD,\textup{in}}}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})r_{i,\textup{2sls}}\right\}\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})r_{i,\textup{2sfe}}\right\}.

Under Assumptions
2
–
3
,
let
A_{i}=Y_{i}-D_{i}\tau_{\textup{c}}
,
\mu_{A}=\E(A_{i})
, and
\bar{A}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}A_{i}
. Let

\displaystyle V_{N}=\begin{pmatrix}V_{\textup{2sls},N}&V_{{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}},N}\\
V_{{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}},N}&V_{\textup{2sfe},N}\end{pmatrix},

where

\displaystyle V_{\textup{2sls},N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(\sigma^{2}_{Z}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c}})^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\var\left\{\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-e)(A_{i}-\mu_{A})\right\},

\displaystyle V_{\textup{2sfe},N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{(\kappa_{N}\cdot\sigma^{2}_{Z}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c}})^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\var\left\{\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})(A_{i}-\bar{A}_{g})\right\},

\displaystyle V_{{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}},N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{\kappa_{N}\cdot(\sigma^{2}_{Z}\cdot\pi_{\textup{c}})^{2}}\cdot\dfrac{1}{N}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\cov\left\{\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-e)(A_{i}-\mu_{A}),\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})(A_{i}-\bar{A}_{g})\right\},

with
\kappa_{N}=\E(S_{Z,\textup{in}})/\sigma^{2}_{Z}
as defined in (
4
). Intuitively,
(V_{N},V_{\textup{2sls},N},V_{\textup{2sfe},N},V_{{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}},N})
define the population analogs of
(\widehat{\Sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}},\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}},\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}},\hat{\sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}})
, respectively, scaled by a factor of
N
.
Let

\Omega_{N}=N^{-1}\cov\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\begin{pmatrix}A_{i}-\mu_{A}\\
Z_{i}(A_{i}-\mu_{A})\\
(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})(A_{i}-\bar{A}_{g})\end{pmatrix}\right\},

and let
\lambda_{\min}(\Omega_{N})
denote the smallest eigenvalue of
\Omega_{N}
.

Theorem 3
.

As
N\to\infty
, if Assumptions
1
–
3
and
5
hold,
\E(Y_{i}^{4})<\infty
, and
\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\Omega_{N})>0
,
then

(i)

\begin{array}[]{r}V_{N}^{-1/2}\cdot\sqrt{N}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\tau_{\textup{c}}\\
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}-\tau_{\textup{c}}\end{pmatrix}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0_{2},I_{2}),\\
V_{N}^{-1/2}(N\widehat{\Sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}})V_{N}^{-1/2}=I_{2}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),\\
\widehat{\Sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}}^{-1/2}\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\tau_{\textup{c}}\\
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}-\tau_{\textup{c}}\end{pmatrix}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0_{2},I_{2}).\end{array}

(ii)

\dfrac{\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}}{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{diff}}}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0,1)
,
where

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{diff}}^{2}&=&\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sls}}+\hat{\textup{se}}^{2}_{\textup{2sfe}}-2\hat{\sigma}_{\textup{2sls},\textup{2sfe}}\\
&=&\displaystyle\dfrac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\left[\dfrac{1}{S_{ZD}}\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})r_{i,\textup{2sls}}\right\}-\dfrac{1}{S_{ZD,\textup{in}}}\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})r_{i,\textup{2sfe}}\right\}\right]^{2}.\end{array}

Theorem
2
implies Proposition
1
and provides explicit forms of the asymptotic variances of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
.
Together, Theorem
2
and Theorem
3
motivate a test of Assumption
3
(homogeneous clusters) based on the
t
-statistic associated with the difference
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
:

t=\dfrac{\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}}{\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{diff}}}.

(18)

In practice, we can compute
t
either using the explicit expression of
\hat{\textup{se}}_{\textup{diff}}
in Theorem
3
(
ii
) or via a cluster bootstrap approximation.
We provide simulation code in the replication files to demonstrate coherence between the two estimates.

5
Simulation

5.1
Validity and efficiency with homogeneous clusters

We first illustrate the validity of the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
under Assumption
3
, along with the impact of covariate adjustment.

We fix the number of clusters at
G=200
and generate the data as follows: (i)
n_{1},\ldots,n_{G}
are i.i.d. Poisson(10); (ii)
e_{1},\ldots,e_{G}
are i.i.d. Uniform(0.4, 0.6); (iii) for
i\in\mathcal{I}
,

•

U_{i}
are i.i.d. with
\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{a})=0.3
,
\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{c})=0.5
,
\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{n})=0.2
;

•

Z_{i}
are independent with
\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=1\mid e_{c(i)})=e_{c(i)}
;

•

D_{i}=1_{\{U_{i}=\textup{a}\}}+Z_{i}\cdot 1_{\{U_{i}=\textup{c}\}}
;

•

X_{i}=(X^{*}_{{c(i)}},X_{i}^{\prime})^{\top}
, where
X_{g}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{X}^{2})
and
X_{i}^{\prime}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
;

•

Y_{i}=D_{i}+X^{*}_{{c(i)}}+X_{i}^{\prime}+\alpha_{{c(i)}}+\epsilon_{i}
, where

\alpha_{g}=X^{*}_{{c(i)}}+\eta_{g}
with
\eta_{g}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\eta}^{2})
, and
\epsilon_{i}
are independent
\mathcal{N}(\mu_{U_{i}},1)
with
(\mu_{\textup{a}},\mu_{\textup{c}},\mu_{\textup{n}})=(2,0,-3)
.

The data-generating process satisfies Assumption
6
with
\tau_{\textup{c}}=1
,
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}=\sigma_{X}^{2}+\sigma_{\eta}^{2}
, and
\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}=4
. The correlation between
D_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}
through
U_{i}
renders
D_{i}
endogenous.

We consider estimation using the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
, indexed by {2sls, 2sfe}, as well as their respective covariate-adjusted counterparts, indexed by {2sls-x, 2sfe-x}, summarized in the third column of Table

.

(i)

Across all settings, the 95% confidence intervals of the four

procedures achieve valid coverage.

(ii)

\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}=4^{-1}(\sigma^{2}_{X}+\sigma^{2}_{\eta})
:

\bullet

(\sigma_{X},\sigma_{\eta})=(1,1)
with

, the two

\bullet

(\sigma_{X},\sigma_{\eta})=(0.5,0.5)
with
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}=0.125
,
2sfe yields a shorter average confidence interval length than 2sls, but 2sls-x outperforms both 2sfe and 2sfe-x due to adjustment for cluster-level covariates.

\bullet

At
(\sigma_{X},\sigma_{\eta})=(0.2,0.2)
with
\sigma^{2}_{\alpha}/\sigma^{2}_{\epsilon}=0.02
, 2sls and 2sls-x without cluster indicators yield shorter average confidence interval lengths than 2sfe and 2sfe-x, respectively.

5.2
Test and estimation with heterogeneous clusters

We now illustrate the properties of the test for cluster heterogeneity.
We fix the number of clusters at
G=100
and the cluster size at
n_{g}=20
for all
g
.
Assume that there are two types of clusters.
The first
50
clusters,
g=1,\ldots,50
, are of the first type and have no cluster-specific effect.
The remaining
50
clusters,
g=51,\ldots,100
, are of the second type and have a cluster-specific effect equal to
\delta
, where
\delta
is a tuning parameter capturing the degree of heterogeneity.
The clusters are homogeneous if and only if
\delta=0
.
Define

\alpha_{g}=1_{\{g>50\}}\delta
for
g=1,\ldots,G
.
When
\delta\neq 0
,
\alpha_{g}
represents heterogeneous cluster-specific effects across different types of clusters.

To facilitate illustration, we assume a constant treatment effect of
\tau=0
for all units. For each cluster
g=1,\ldots,G
, we generate
(Z_{i},D_{i},Y_{i})
for
{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}
as follows:

•

U_{i}
are i.i.d. with
\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{c})=0.7
and
\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{n})=0.3
;

•

Z_{i}
are independent
\textup{Bernoulli}(e_{g})
, where
e_{g}=1/\{1+\exp(-0.5\alpha_{g})\}
;

•

D_{i}=Z_{i}\cdot 1_{\{U_{i}=\textup{c}\}}
;

•

Y_{i}=Y_{i}(1)=Y_{i}(0)=\alpha_{g}+\epsilon_{i}
, where

\epsilon_{i}\overset{\textup{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

The definition of
e_{g}
implies that clusters with larger
\alpha_{g}
have higher IV probabilities, so the IV distributions are also heterogeneous across clusters.

Figure
1
shows the distributions of the
t
-statistic we proposed in (
18
) based on 1,000 independent replications for
\delta=0,1,2
.
For the case
\delta=0
, we additionally overlay the standard normal density on the histogram.
The table below reports the average values of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
across the three cases.

(a) Homogeneous clusters

(b) Heterogeneous clusters

(c) Heterogeneous clusters

\delta=0

\delta=1

\delta=2

Ave.
\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}\\
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0.0004\\
0.0008\end{pmatrix}

Ave.
\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}\\
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0.175\\
-0.002\end{pmatrix}

Ave.
\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}\\
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0.695\\
-0.0005\end{pmatrix}

Figure 1
:
Distributions of the
t
-statistic we proposed in (
18
) under homogeneous and heterogeneous clusters over 1,000 independent replications. The curve in Figure
1
(a) represents the standard normal density.
The table below reports the corresponding average values of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
.

When
\delta=0
, we have
\alpha_{g}=0
and
e_{g}=0.5
for all
g
, corresponding to homogeneous clusters.
The resulting empirical distribution closely resembles the standard normal distribution, and 95.4% of the
t
-statistics satisfy
|t|\leq 1.96
. Both observations are coherent with the theoretical results in Theorem
3
, supporting the validity of the test.
In addition, the averages of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
are both close to the true value,
\tau=0
, coherent with the theoretical results in Proposition
1
.

The cases
\delta=1
and
\delta=2
introduce heterogeneity between the first and last 50 clusters, thereby violating Assumption
3
.
When
\delta=1
, the corresponding empirical distribution is centered around
4.93
, deviating substantially from the standard normal distribution.
99.7% of the
t
-statistics satisfy
|t|>1.96
, illustrating the power of the test under violations of Assumption
3
.
When
\delta=2
, the corresponding empirical distribution is centered around 10.23, deviating even further from the standard normal distribution.
100% of the
t
-statistics satisfy
|t|>1.96
, indicating increased power as the degree of heterogeneity increases.
In both cases, the average values of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
are close to the true value,
\tau=0
, whereas those of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
exhibit clear biases.

6
Application

We now illustrate our methods using data from a randomized evaluation of a microcredit program introduced in rural Morocco in 2006
(
Crépon et al. 2015
)
.
The sample includes 5,898 households (units) nested within 162 villages.
Villages were matched into 81 pairs based on observable characteristics, and within each pair, one village was randomly assigned to treatment, and the other to control.
After randomization, credit agents from the partner microfinance institution started to promote microcredit in treatment villages.

Following
Crépon et al. 2015
,
we focus on estimating the impact of microcredit take-up on outcomes using the subset of households in the whole sample with high probability to borrow, as predicted by
Crépon et al. 2015
’s propensity score model.
The outcome of interest,
Y_{i}
, is total household production from agriculture, livestock, and non-agricultural business; see “Sales and home consumption” in
Crépon et al. 2015
.
The treatment
D_{i}
is an indicator of microcredit take-up at endline.
The IV
Z_{i}
is an indicator of residence in a treatment village.
Covariates
X_{i}
include household size, number of adults, age of the household head, baseline borrowing, and other household characteristics.
In total, 2,448 households (49.6%) in the sample reside in treated villages (
Z_{i}=1
), and 410 households (8.3%) took up microcredit (
D_{i}=1
).
Following
Crépon et al. 2015
, we define each
village pair
as a stratum (cluster) when constructing the cluster indicators
C_{i}
.
The
t
-statistic from the cluster heterogeneity test is 0.183, so that we can not reject Assumption
3
.

In addition to the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
and their covariate-adjusted counterparts, we also consider their
ols
counterparts, indexed by {ols, fe, ols-x, fe-x}, summarized in the fourth column of Table
1
.

:
Violin plots of the cluster bootstrap distributions of the eight point estimators over 1,000 replications. The width of each violin represents the empirical probability density, with wider sections indicating higher data concentration. Circles (
\circ
) denote point estimates from the original data.
Error bars indicate 95% confidence intervals computed from the cluster-robust standard errors.

Figure
2
illustrates the point estimates from the eight procedures in Table
1
, along with 95% confidence intervals using cluster-robust standard errors and their cluster bootstrap distributions over 1,000 replications.
The four
ols
procedures (ols, fe, ols-x, fe-x) return smaller point estimates than their
2sls
counterparts, and are therefore likely biased.
The table below reports the point estimates, associated cluster-robust standard errors, and 95% confidence intervals from the four
2sls
procedures.
The covariate-adjusted
2sfe
(2sfe-x) gives the smallest cluster-robust standard error and the narrowest confidence interval.
Note that the confidence interval associated with the unadjusted canonical
2sls
includes zero, whereas the other three confidence intervals do not. This illustrates the efficiency gains from covariate adjustment and the inclusion of cluster indicators.

7
Discussion

We examined the canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
as two approaches to IV analysis for clustered data when both the treatment and IV vary within clusters.
Our main contributions are threefold.

First, under homogeneous clusters, we established the validity of both procedures, and compared their relative efficiency when the potential outcomes model includes cluster-specific effects.
Our results caution against the routine inclusion of cluster indicators without considering efficiency implications.

Second, under heterogeneous clusters, we showed that the point estimator from
2sfe
recovers a weighted average of cluster-specific LATEs, whereas that from the canonical
2sls
generally does not. This suggests an advantage of
2sfe
when analyzing data nested within heterogeneous clusters, such as stores in different geographic locations or firms of differing sizes.

Finally, motivated by the distinct asymptotic behavior of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}-\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
under homogeneous and heterogeneous clusters, we developed an asymptotic theory for the joint distribution of
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}}
when clusters are homogeneous, and proposed a test for cluster heterogeneity.

We summarize the trade-off between canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
as follows:

Canonical
2sls

2sfe

Cluster-constant treatment, IV, covariates

Yes

No

Requires cluster-robust standard errors?

Yes

Yes

Efficiency gain from cluster-level covariates?

Yes

No

Handles heterogeneous clusters?

No

Yes

Efficiency

Under Assumption
6
,
2sfe
is more efficient than

the canonical
2sls
if and only if (
9
) holds.

Ordinary least squares as a special case.

Ordinary least squares is a special case of
2sls
with
Z_{i}=D_{i}
.
Therefore, our theory encompasses the comparison between the
ols
regressions

\texttt{ols}(Y_{i}\sim 1+D_{i})=\texttt{2sls}(Y_{i}\sim 1+D_{i}\mid 1+D_{i})
and
\texttt{ols}(Y_{i}\sim D_{i}+C_{i})=\texttt{2sls}(Y_{i}\sim D_{i}+C_{i}\mid D_{i}+C_{i})

under cluster sampling when the treatment
D_{i}
is as-if randomly assigned within each cluster.

summarizes the key notation.

Section
S4
provides the proofs of the results in Section
S2
.

Sections
S5
–
S6
provide the proofs of the results in the main paper.

S1
Notation

S1.1
General notation

For positive integer
m
,
let
1_{m}
and
0_{m}
denote the
m\times 1
vectors of ones and zeros, respectively; let

I_{m}
denote the
m\times m
identity matrix,

J_{m}=1_{m}1_{m}^{\top}
denote the
m\times m
matrix of ones, and

P_{m}=I_{m}-m^{-1}J_{m}
denote the
m\times m
projection matrix with
P_{m}^{2}=P_{m}
and
P_{m}1_{m}=0_{m}
.
Let
\|\cdot\|
denote the Euclidean norm, with
\|a\|=(a^{\top}a)^{1/2}
for a vector
a
.
For matrices
A_{1},\ldots,A_{K}
, let
\diag(A_{1},\ldots,A_{K})
and
\diag(A_{k})_{k=1,\ldots,K}
denote the block diagonal matrix with
A_{1},\ldots,A_{K}
on the diagonal.
Let
\lambda_{\min}(\cdot)
denote the smallest eigenvalue of a symmetric matrix.
Let
\|\cdot\|_{2}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\textsc{f}}
denote the spectral norm and Frobenius norm.
For any matrix
B
, we have

\|B\|_{2}\leq\|B\|_{\textsc{f}}\leq\sqrt{\textup{rank}(B)}\cdot\|B\|_{2}.

(S1)

For sequences of vectors or matrices
(a_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
, we use
a_{n}=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
to denote componentwise convergence in probability to zero, and
a_{n}=O(1)
and
a_{n}=O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
to denote componentwise boundedness and boundedness in probability.

OLS
and 2
SLS
.

For
u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
v_{i},w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
defined for
i\in\mathcal{I}
, denote by

•

\texttt{ols}(u_{i}\sim v_{i})
: the
ols
regression of
u_{i}
on
v_{i}
over
i\in\mathcal{I}

\texttt{coef}(u_{i}\sim v_{i})
: the corresponding coefficient vector/matrix of
v_{i}

\texttt{res}(u_{i}\sim v_{i})=u_{i}-v_{i}^{\top}\texttt{coef}(u_{i}\sim v_{i})
: the
ols
residual

•

\texttt{2sls}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})
: the
2sls
regression of
u_{i}
on
v_{i}
instrumented by
w_{i}
over
i\in\mathcal{I}

\texttt{coef}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})
: the corresponding coefficient vector/matrix of
v_{i}

\texttt{res}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})=u_{i}-v_{i}^{\top}\texttt{coef}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})
: the IV residual

Linear projection.

For a random variable
Y\in\mathbb{R}
and a random vector
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let

\proj(Y\mid X)
denote the linear projection of
Y
onto
X
, defined as
\proj(Y\mid X)=b_{0}^{\top}X
, where
b_{0}=\argmin_{b\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\E\{(Y-b^{\top}X)^{2}\}=\{\E(XX^{\top})\}^{-1}\E(XY)
;
let

\displaystyle\res(Y\mid X)=Y-\proj(Y\mid X)

denote the corresponding projection residual.

Clustered population.

For a scalar
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
defined for
i\in\mathcal{I}=\{gj:g=1,\ldots,G;\,j=1,\ldots,n_{g}\}
, denote by

•

\bar{a}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
: the population average;

•

\bar{a}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
: the within-cluster average for cluster
g
;

•

a_{[g]}=(a_{g1},\ldots,a_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}=(a_{i}:{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{g}}
: the
n_{g}\times 1
vector of
a_{i}
for units within cluster
g
;

•

a=(a_{[1]}^{\top},\ldots,a_{[G]}^{\top})^{\top}=(a_{11},\ldots,a_{G,{n_{G}}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
: the
N\times 1
vector of
a_{i}
for all units ordered by clusters;

•

\dot{a}_{i}=a_{i}-\bar{a}_{g}\in\mathbb{R}
: the
cluster-centered
variant of
a_{i}
;

•

\dot{a}_{[g]}=(\dot{a}_{g1},\ldots,\dot{a}_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}=a_{[g]}-1_{n_{g}}\bar{a}_{g}=P_{n_{g}}a_{[g]}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{g}\times 1}
: the cluster-centered variant of
a_{[g]}
.

We can view the accent “
\,\cdot\,
” in
\dot{a}_{[g]}
as a linear operator with
\dot{a}_{[g]}=P_{n_{g}}a_{[g]}
for all
n_{g}\times 1
vectors
a_{[g]}
.
A useful fact is

\displaystyle\texttt{res}(a_{i}\sim 1)=a_{i}-\bar{a},\quad\texttt{res}(a_{i}\sim C_{i})=\dot{a}_{i}.

(S2)

The above notation extends to vector variables
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
after replacing “vector” by “matrix” with the corresponding dimensions.

For scalars
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
defined on
i\in\mathcal{I}
, we have

\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}\dot{a}_{i}\dot{b}_{i}=(P_{n_{g}}a_{[g]})^{\top}(P_{n_{g}}b_{[g]})=(a_{[g]}^{\top}P_{n_{g}})\cdot b_{[g]}=\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}\dot{a}_{i}b_{i}.

(S3)

S1.2
Main paper-specific notation

Let
Y_{{[g]}}=(Y_{g1},\ldots,Y_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}
,
D_{{[g]}}=(D_{g1},\ldots,D_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}
, and
Z_{{[g]}}=(Z_{g1},\ldots,Z_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}
. Let

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{lllllllll}Y=\begin{pmatrix}Y_{[1]}\\
\vdots\\
Y_{[G]}\end{pmatrix},&D=\begin{pmatrix}D_{[1]}\\
\vdots\\
D_{[G]}\end{pmatrix},&Z=\begin{pmatrix}Z_{[1]}\\
\vdots\\
Z_{[G]}\end{pmatrix},&X=\begin{pmatrix}X_{[1]}\\
\vdots\\
X_{[G]}\end{pmatrix},\\
\bar{Y}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}Y_{i},&\bar{D}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}D_{i},&\bar{Z}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}Z_{i},&\bar{X}_{g}=n_{g}^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}X_{i},\\
\dot{Y}_{[g]}=Y_{{[g]}}-1_{n_{g}}\bar{Y}_{g},&\dot{D}_{[g]}=D_{{[g]}}-1_{n_{g}}\bar{D}_{g},&\dot{Z}_{[g]}=Z_{{[g]}}-1_{n_{g}}\bar{Z}_{g},&\dot{X}_{[g]}=X_{{[g]}}-1_{n_{g}}\bar{X}_{g}.\end{array}

Let

\displaystyle S_{Z,\textup{in}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{{c(i)}})^{2}=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\dot{Z}_{i}^{2},

\displaystyle S_{ZY,\textup{in}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{{c(i)}})(Y_{i}-\bar{Y}_{{c(i)}})=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\dot{Z}_{i}\dot{Y}_{i},

\displaystyle S_{ZD,\textup{in}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{{c(i)}})(D_{i}-\bar{D}_{{c(i)}})=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\dot{Z}_{i}\dot{D}_{i}.

Let

\displaystyle S_{Z}=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})^{2},\quad S_{Z,g}=\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z}_{g})^{2}=\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}\dot{Z}_{i}^{2},

(S8)

with
S_{Z,\textup{in}}=N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}S_{Z,g}.

Let

S_{ZY}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})(Y_{i}-\bar{Y}),\quad S_{ZD}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}(Z_{i}-\bar{Z})(D_{i}-\bar{D}).

Notation under Assumptions
2
–
3
.

Under Assumption
3
(identical marginal distribution), let

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{l}\begin{array}[]{l}e=\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=1)=\E(Z_{i}),\quad\sigma^{2}_{Z}=\var(Z_{i})=e(1-e),\end{array}\\
\begin{array}[]{llll}\mu_{Y}=\E(Y_{i}),&\mu_{ZY}=\E(Z_{i}Y_{i}),&\mu_{D}=\E(D_{i}),\quad\mu_{ZD}=\E(Z_{i}D_{i}),\\
\mu_{X}=\E(X_{i}),&V_{X}=\cov(X_{i}),&\mu_{XX}=\E(X_{i}X_{i}^{\top})=V_{X}+\mu_{X}\mu_{X}^{\top},\\
\mu_{XZ}=\E(X_{i}Z_{i}),&\mu_{XD}=\E(X_{i}D_{i}),\\
\pi_{\textup{a}}=\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{a}),&\pi_{\textup{c}}=\mathbb{P}(U_{i}=\textup{c})\end{array}\end{array}

be shorthand notation for moments of the common distribution.

Under Assumptions
2
–
3
(IV and identical marginal distribution),
\pi_{\textup{c}}>0
so that
\tau_{\textup{c}}=\E(\tau_{i}\mid U_{i}=\textup{c})
is well defined. Let

A_{i}=Y_{i}-D_{i}\tau_{\textup{c}},\quad\mu_{A}=\E(A_{i})=\mu_{Y}-\mu_{D}\tau_{\textup{c}},\quad\dot{A}_{i}=\dot{Y}_{i}-\dot{D}_{i}\tau_{\textup{c}}.

(S17)

Let
\proj(A_{i}\mid 1,X_{i})
denote the linear projection of
A_{i}
on
(1,X_{i})
. Let

\begin{array}[]{l}\beta_{X,A}=\{\cov(X_{i})\}^{-1}\cov(X_{i},A_{i}),\\
R_{i,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}}=\res(A_{i}\mid 1,X_{i})=A_{i}-\proj(A_{i}\mid 1,X_{i})\end{array}

(S18)

denote the coefficient vector of
X_{i}
and the corresponding residual, with

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\proj(A_{i}\mid 1,X_{i})&=&\mu_{A}+(X_{i}-\mu_{X})^{\top}\beta_{X,A}\\
&=&\beta_{1}+X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{X,A},\\
R_{i,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}}&=&A_{i}-\beta_{1}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{X,A}\\
&=&Y_{i}-\beta_{1}-D_{i}\tau_{\textup{c}}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{X,A},\end{array}

(S19)

where
\beta_{1}=\mu_{A}-\mu_{X}^{\top}\beta_{X,A}
.

Let

\begin{array}[]{lll}S_{X,\textup{in}}=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\dot{X}_{i}\dot{X}^{\top}_{i},&&S_{XA,\textup{in}}=\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\dot{X}_{i}\dot{A}_{i},\\
\gamma_{X,A}=\{\E(S_{X,\textup{in}})\}^{-1}\E(S_{XA,\textup{in}}),&&R_{i,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}}=\dot{A}_{i}-\dot{X}^{\top}_{i}\gamma_{X,A}.\end{array}

(S20)

S2
Complete theory for
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}

We provide in this section the complete theory for
(\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}},\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}})
from the covariate-adjusted canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
, including details on the asymptotic conditions.
As a foundation, we review in Section
S2.1
the asymptotic framework and theory for sample mean and
2sls
with clustered units from
Hansen and
Lee 2019
.
To simplify notation, we follow
Hansen and
Lee 2019
in omitting the sample size
N
from the subscript when no confusion is likely to arise.

S2.1
Review of
Hansen and
Lee 2019

Assumption
S1
below reviews
Hansen and
Lee 2019
on cluster sizes for the central limit theorem for the sample mean of clustered units. Assumption
5
is the most restrictive case of Assumption
S1
with
r=2
.

Assumption S1
.

As
N\to\infty
,

(i)

there exists a constant
r\in[2,\infty)
so that
\limsup_{N\to\infty}N^{-1}(\sum_{g=1}^{G}n_{g}^{r})^{2/r}<\infty
;

(ii)

\displaystyle\max_{g=1,\ldots,G}\,{n_{g}^{2}}/N\to 0
.

Lemma
S1
below reviews the asymptotic theory for sample mean in
Hansen and
Lee 2019
that allows for heterogeneous clusters.

Lemma S1
.

Let
B_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a
p\times 1
random vector defined for
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Let
\bar{B}_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}B_{i}
denote the sample mean,
\Omega_{N}=\cov(\sqrt{N}\bar{B}_{N})
denote the covariance matrix of
\sqrt{N}\bar{B}_{N}
.
Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{N}=N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\widetilde{B}_{g}\widetilde{B}_{g}^{\top},\quad\text{where}\ \ \widetilde{B}_{g}=\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}B_{i}.

Assume Assumption
1
. As
N\to\infty
,

(i)

(
Hansen and
Lee 2019
, Theorem 1, weak law of large numbers)
: If

•

\displaystyle\max_{g=1,\ldots,G}n_{g}/N\to 0
;

•

Uniform integrability:

\lim_{M\to\infty}\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E\left(\|B_{i}\|\cdot 1_{\{\|B_{i}\|>M\}}\right)=0,

(S21)

then
\|\bar{B}_{N}-\E(\bar{B}_{N})\|=o_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
.

(ii)

(
Hansen and
Lee 2019
, Theorem 2–3, central limit theorem)
: If

•

Assumption
S1
holds for a constant
r\in[2,\infty)
;

•

Uniform integrability: For the constant
r
from Assumption
S1
,

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E\left(\|B_{i}\|^{r}\cdot 1_{\{\|B_{i}\|>M\}}\right)=0;

(S22)

•

\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\Omega_{N})>0
,

then

\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\cdot\sqrt{N}\left\{\bar{B}_{N}-\E(\bar{B}_{N})\right\}\rightsquigarrow\mathcal{N}(0_{p},I_{p}).

Further assume that
\E(B_{i})=0
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
. Then

\displaystyle\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}\widetilde{\Omega}_{N}\Omega_{N}^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{p}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{N}^{-1/2}\cdot\sqrt{N}\left\{\bar{B}_{N}-\E(\bar{B}_{N})\right\}

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(0_{p},I_{p}).

Remark S1
.

Conditions (
S21
) and (
S22
) require uniform integrability of
\|B_{i}\|
and
\|B_{i}\|^{r}
, respectively, accommodating different marginal distributions of
B_{i}
.
When
B_{i}
are identically distributed, they simplify to
\E(\|B_{i}\|)<\infty
and
\E(\|B_{i}\|^{r})<\infty
, respectively.
With distributional heterogeneity,

•

a sufficient condition for (
S21
) is
\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(\|X_{i}\|^{s})<\infty
for some
s>1

•

a sufficient condition for (
S22
) is
\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(\|X_{i}\|^{s})<\infty
for some
s\in(r,\infty)
;

see Lemma
S7
(
iv
).

Lemma
S2
below reviews the asymptotic theory for general
2sls
in
Hansen and
Lee 2019
in the context of just-identified cases.

Setting of
Hansen and
Lee 2019
.

For
i\in\mathcal{I}
, let

u_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
denote the dependent variable,

v_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote the
p\times 1
regressor vector,
and
w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote the
p\times 1
IV vector, respectively.
Let

\displaystyle u_{[g]}=(u_{g1},\ldots,u_{g,n_{g}})^{\top},\quad V_{[g]}=(v_{g1},\ldots,v_{g,n_{g}})^{\top},\quad W_{[g]}=(w_{g1},\ldots,w_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}

(S23)

denote the concatenations of
(u_{i},v_{i},w_{i})
, respectively, for units within cluster
g
.
Assume that

\displaystyle u_{[g]}=V_{[g]}\beta+\epsilon_{{[g]}},\quad V_{[g]}=W_{[g]}\gamma+\delta_{[g]},\quad\E(W_{[g]}^{\top}\epsilon_{[g]})=0,

(S24)

where
\epsilon_{[g]}=(\epsilon_{g1},\ldots,\epsilon_{g,n_{g}})^{\top}
is an
n_{g}\times 1
error vector with
W_{[g]}^{\top}\epsilon_{[g]}=\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}W_{i}\epsilon_{i}
.

Let
\hat{\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
denote the estimated coefficient vector of
v_{i}
from
\texttt{2sls}(u_{i}\sim v_{i}\mid w_{i})
, as the
2sls
estimator for
\beta
.
Let
\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}=N\widehat{\cov}(\hat{\beta})
denote the cluster-robust covariance of
\hat{\beta}
scaled by a factor of
N
; c.f. Definition
1
.

Let

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{rclcl}\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}&=&\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\E(W_{[g]}^{\top}V_{[g]})\overset{\eqref{eq:vw_hansen}}{=}\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(w_{i}v_{i}^{\top}),\\
\displaystyle\Psi_{N}&=&\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{g=1}^{G}\E(W_{[g]}^{\top}W_{[g]})\overset{\eqref{eq:vw_hansen}}{=}\displaystyle N^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(w_{i}w_{i}^{\top}),\\
\displaystyle\Omega_{N}&=&N^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\E(W_{[g]}^{\top}\epsilon_{{[g]}}\epsilon_{{[g]}}^{\top}W_{[g]})\overset{\eqref{eq:model_hansen}}{=}N^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\cov(W_{[g]}^{\top}\epsilon_{{[g]}})=N^{-1}\displaystyle\sum_{g=1}^{G}\cov\left(\displaystyle\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}w_{i}\epsilon_{i}\right),\\
\Sigma_{N}&=&(\Gamma_{N}^{\top}\Psi_{N}^{-1}\Gamma_{N})^{-1}\Gamma_{N}^{\top}\Psi_{N}^{-1}\Omega_{N}\Psi_{N}^{-1}\Gamma_{N}(\Gamma_{N}^{\top}\Psi_{N}^{-1}\Gamma_{N})^{-1}=\Gamma_{N}^{-1}\Omega_{N}(\Gamma_{N}^{\top})^{-1},\end{array}

where

-

the equivalent forms of
(\Gamma_{N},\Psi_{N})
follow from
W_{[g]}^{\top}V_{[g]}=\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}w_{i}v_{i}^{\top}
and
W_{[g]}^{\top}W_{[g]}=\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}w_{i}w_{i}^{\top}
by the definition of
(V_{[g]},W_{[g]})
in (
S23
);

-

the equivalent form of
\Omega_{N}
follows from
\E(W_{[g]}^{\top}\epsilon_{{[g]}})=0
and
W_{[g]}^{\top}\epsilon_{{[g]}}=\sum_{{i\in\mathcal{I}_{g}}}W_{i}\epsilon_{i}
by (
S24
).

-

the equivalent form of
\Sigma_{N}
follows from
(\Gamma_{N}^{\top}\Psi_{N}^{-1}\Gamma_{N})^{-1}=\Gamma_{N}^{-1}\Psi_{N}(\Gamma_{N}^{\top})^{-1}
for just-identified cases.

Lemma S2
.

(
Hansen and
Lee 2019
, Theorem 9)
If as
N\to\infty
,

(i)

Assumption
1
holds;

(ii)

Assumption
S1
holds for a constant
r\in[2,\infty)
;

(iii)

\Gamma_{N}
has full rank
p
;

(iv)

\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\Psi_{N})>0
,
\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\Omega_{N})>0
;

(v)

there exists a constant
s\in[r,\infty)
so that

\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(|u_{i}|^{2s})<\infty,\quad\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(\|v_{i}\|^{2s})<\infty,\quad\displaystyle\sup_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\E(\|w_{i}\|^{2s})<\infty;

(vi)

either

•

(u_{i},v_{i},w_{i})
have identical marginal distributions; or

•

r<s
,

then for any sequence of full-rank
p\times q
matrices
C_{N}
,

\displaystyle(C_{N}^{\top}\Sigma_{N}C_{N}^{\top})^{-1/2}C_{N}^{\top}\sqrt{N}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(0_{q},I_{q}),

\displaystyle(C_{N}^{\top}\Sigma_{N}C_{N}^{\top})^{-1/2}C_{N}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}C_{N}(C_{N}^{\top}\Sigma_{N}C_{N}^{\top})^{-1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{q}+o_{\mathbb{P}}(1),

\displaystyle(C_{N}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}C_{N}^{\top})^{-1/2}C_{N}^{\top}\sqrt{N}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)

\displaystyle\rightsquigarrow

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(0_{q},I_{q}).

The standard errors for elements of
C_{N}^{\top}\hat{\beta}
can be estimated by taking the square roots of the diagonal elements of
N^{-1}C_{N}^{\top}\widehat{\Sigma}_{N}C_{N}^{\top}
.

S2.2
Central limit theorems for
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}
and
\hat{\tau}_{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}

Assumption
S2
below states the regularity condition we assume in our theory for the covariate-adjusted canonical
2sls
and
2sfe
.

Recall from (
S18
)–(
S20
) that under Assumptions
2
–
3
,

R_{i,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}}=\res(A_{i}\mid 1,X_{i})=Y_{i}-\beta_{1}-D_{i}\tau_{\textup{c}}-X_{i}^{\top}\beta_{X,A},\quad R_{i,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}}=\dot{A}_{i}-\dot{X}^{\top}_{i}\gamma_{X,A},

where
A_{i}=Y_{i}-D_{i}\tau_{\textup{c}}
and
\gamma_{X,A}=\{\E(S_{X,\textup{in}})\}^{-1}\E(S_{XA,\textup{in}})
.
Let

\Omega_{N,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}}=N^{-1}\cov\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\begin{pmatrix}1\\
Z_{i}\\
X_{i}\end{pmatrix}R_{i,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}}\right\},\quad\Omega_{N,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}}=N^{-1}\cov\left\{\displaystyle\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\begin{pmatrix}\dot{Z}_{i}\\
\dot{X}_{i}\end{pmatrix}R_{i,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}}\right\}.

(S30)

Assumption S2
.

As
N\to\infty
,

(i)

Assumptions
1
–
3
and
5
hold;

(ii)

\E(Y_{i}^{4})<\infty

(iii)

\E(\|X_{i}\|^{4})<\infty
;
\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\Omega_{N,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}})>0
;

(iv)

there exists a constant
s>2
such that
\E(\|X_{i}\|^{2s})<\infty
;

\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\Omega_{N,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}})>0
;
\inf_{N}\lambda_{\min}(\E(S_{X,\textup{in}}))>0
.

Assumption
S2
(
i
) ensures that
\Omega_{N,{\textup{2sls}\textup{-x}}}
and
\Omega_{N,{\textup{2sfe}\textup{-x}}}
in (
S30
) are well defined.
We assume
r=2
to simplify the proofs.
By
Hansen and
Lee 2019
, this can be relaxed to
r\in[2,\infty)
